[^189]: The 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan) reads "পরশ পররশ" (paraśa pararaśa); understood as paraspara rasa, "rasa on one another"; possibly "the touch — the highest rasa." The sense is unclear.
[^190]: "Coming near Ai" is āika niyare (āika — "of Ai," that is, of Shachi, in the Brajabuli genitive). The word vihi (so in the 1912/13 ed., checked against the scan: "অঞ্জলি ভরি ভরি বিহি"; in brackets in the 1960 edition) is understood as vidhi, "duly, according to rite"; possibly "the Creator." The champaka buds are the women's joined fingers.
[^191]: The dark hair of the bowing, golden-bodied women evokes a cloud with lightning. The 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan) reads "সজল জলদ কির, চর তছু ভাঁতি"; the 1960 ed. has thira-cara, understood as "now still, now moving" (the reading of A remains unclear even on the scan). "Dark" is asita (A, on the scan: "অসিত সুকাঁতি"; the 1960 edition has amita, "immeasurable").
[^192]: Tudi (tuḍi) is apparently the same as raga Todi (1403, 1407).
[^193]: The songs 1419–1444 continue the theme of the feelings of the women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava; see the note on 12.1404); the Gaudiya Vaishnava tradition (Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati) does not accept gaura-nagari. Madana is Kamadeva, the god of love.
[^194]: The women's rites (stri-achara) are wedding rites performed by women according to family and local custom.
[^195]: āiha-suiha — married women whose husbands are living (Bengali eyo); only they perform the auspicious wedding rites.
[^196]: Shashthi is a goddess, the protectress of children; her worship is part of Bengali wedding rites. "The place of Shashthi" (ṣaṣṭhī-thala) is the place of her worship.
[^197]: "Sat" renders বৈসে (baise): so in the 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan: "কেবা, বৈসে প্রবেশিয়া ভবন-মাঝে"); baine, naige, haile in the OCR are recognition errors.
[^198]: Purandara is Indra, the king of the gods.
[^199]: "পরশপর পহুঁ চরিত ভনি অনিবার মুদমতি গতি নয়ী" — so in the 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan); the sense of mudamati-gati nayī is unclear, the translation tentative.
[^200]: Rhythm syllables of the drums; the 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan) reads "থোঙ্গ থোঙ্কণ ঝিকি কু ঝাঙ্কিট, ঠিঠ্ঠি টন ন ন ন নায়ে"; the 1960 ed. (OCR) thoda thoskaṇa biki ku jhānkiṭa, ṭhiṭhaṭhi ṭana nanalananāye. "Skilled" renders sughara (A, on the scan: "সুঘর বাদক বৃন্দ").
[^201]: Ulu (ulu-dhvani) is the auspicious prolonged cry of Bengali women at festivities. "Brahmanas" is bhūsura in the original, "gods on earth."
[^202]: "In a suitable raga" — yathā-rāga (likewise in 1477, 1481, 1487, 1491, 1495); the raga is not named.
[^203]: The lord of the gods is Indra; Shachi here is his consort (the namesake of the Lord's mother).
[^204]: "Ulu lu lu" (ulu) — the jubilant cry of Bengali women at a wedding and other auspicious events.
[^205]: Here and in 1488 (and in the songs 1499–1510), again the feelings of the women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404.
[^206]: Aiha-suiha (āiha-suiha; cf. Bengali eyo) — married women whose husbands are living; only they perform the auspicious wedding rites.
[^207]: "To take upon oneself the ills (balai)" — an expression of tender love: "may all the ills that threaten you fall on me."
[^208]: Nichhani — a rite in which something (or oneself) is "given away" for a beloved to ward off misfortune from him; here the full moon itself is only such an offering for that face.
[^209]: "Sets out" renders করত বিজয় (karata vijaya): so in the 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan) and in the 1960 ed.; "কানন," "ধর দির" in the OCR are recognition errors. The songs 1491–1498 are in Brajabuli.
[^210]: The list of musical instruments is given in transliteration. The 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan) reads "শৃঙ্গিকাক বিলাস কংসারি… মঞ্জু কুণ্ডলী বারুণা" — rendered "shringika, kaka-vilasa"; "বারুণা" (bāruṇā) is unidentified. The last line consists of drum-rhythm syllables (bols); on the scan of A: "তক থোঙ্গ থোঙ্গিন, তক থবিকু তক তক থুনা".
[^211]: "Dhi dhi kata…" are dance-rhythm syllables (bols); the 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan) reads "ধি ধি কট ধা ধিনি নি নি নি দৃক্কুতা দৃমিত কথঈ". Ghanashyama is the poet's signature (Narahari Chakravarti's other name). The end of the line on the scan of A is "শ্যাম হিয় উমতা অঈ" (umatā aī, "enraptured").
[^212]: The four kinds of instruments: tata (stringed), ghana (solid — cymbals, gongs), anaddha (covered with skin — drums), shushira (wind).
[^213]: Lasya is a gentle, graceful dance. The rhythm syllables (bols) follow the 1912/13 ed. (checked against the scan: "ভণ তক তক থৈ থৈ থৈ, আই অতি নি নি নি তিয়া"); "আই জাতি" in the OCR of copy B is an error.
[^214]: The Thousand-faced One is Ananta (Shesha); Vidhi is Brahma; Mahesha is Shiva; Girija is Parvati. In A/B the words "বিহি মহেশ" are erroneously repeated.
[^215]: "In a suitable raga" — yathā-rāga (likewise in 1503, 1507, 1511, 1527, 1531); the raga is not named.
[^216]: The songs 1499–1510 again depict the feelings of the women of Nadia ("nagaris," 1507) for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404.
[^217]: Jhapa (jhāpā) — a golden hair ornament (a pendant for the braid).
[^218]: Gandharaja — a fragrant white flower (gardenia); champa (champaka) — a tree with fragrant golden-yellow flowers.
[^219]: Nichhani — a rite in which something (or oneself) is "given away" for a beloved to ward off misfortune from him; here hundreds of Kamadevas give themselves as a ransom for that beauty.
[^220]: "As if" — "মেন" (mena) in all copies; reading unclear (OCR), understood as "যেন" (yena).
[^221]: The bridal chamber — basara-ghara: the room where on the wedding night the women of the house surround the couple with jokes and songs.
[^222]: Chhodla (chhoḍalā; likewise in 1536) — reading and meaning unclear; apparently the place of the wedding rite, a platform under a canopy (cf. Bengali chhādnātalā).
[^223]: Aiha-suiha (āiha-suiha) — married women whose husbands are living; only they perform the auspicious wedding rites.
[^224]: "Going" — by sense; "উরুথিয়া" (uruthiyā) in all copies — reading unclear (OCR). Circling the groom seven times with a lamp is the rite of welcoming the groom (varana, 1526).
[^225]: "Wedding platform": *chhoḍalā* (cf. the same word earlier in the description of the wedding), apparently the place of the wedding rite where bride and groom stand. The meaning is conjectural.
[^226]: Reading unclear (OCR): "bājata thara māhi atula" (A/B "nāhi atula"); "bracelets" follows G *kaṅkaṇa*(?), A/B read *kamuka* (possibly *kanaka*, "golden luster").
[^227]: "To Krishna" follows B (*kṛṣṇe*); in the OCR of the 1960 ed. the word is garbled, and A lacks this line. Narahari calls Gaura Krishna.
[^228]: "Narahari's Lord" (*narahari-nātha*): the Lord of the poet Narahari, i.e. Gaura; the poet's usual signature in a pada (likewise in 1570, 1574).
[^229]: "Bridal chamber": *bāsara-ghara*, the room in the bride's house where the newlyweds spend the first night surrounded by the women of the family, who joke with the groom.
[^230]: "As if": reading unclear (OCR): G *senā*, A/B *menā*; probably *yena*, "as if."
[^231]: "Reddish feet" follows A/B *rāṅgā pāya*; the 1960 ed. reads *rādhā pāya* ("at Radha's feet"), possibly an OCR error.
[^232]: Kushandika: the rite before the sacred fire that concludes the wedding (performed after the wedding night).
[^233]: Following A/B *karite*; the 1960 ed. reads *kahite*: "Who can describe the longing of them all?"
[^234]: "A great tumult": reading unclear (OCR): G *bhūggara*, B *ḍūmbara*; taken as *ḍambara* ("noise, pomp; a multitude"). The same word in 1612 ("a vast throng"): G *bhūsmara*, B *tusthara*.
[^235]: The names of the instruments are partly unclear (OCR): *trisari* (B *bhrilari*), *kuṇḍalī*, *pula*. The drum syllables follow B ("kuku nunu nunu, nu dhā"); G is garbled ("buku huma, su sudhā").
[^236]: Godhuli: dusk, the "hour of cow-dust," when the herds return home. The Suradhuni ("river of the gods") is the Ganga.
[^237]: That is, He accepted initiation from him (CB Adi-khanda). On Ishvara Puri see 12.1365.
[^238]: Shriman Pandita is an associate of the Lord in Navadvipa; he was among the first to whom the Lord, on returning from Gaya, told of the pilgrimage (CB Adi-khanda).
[^239]: Vishnupada ("the foot of Vishnu") is the temple at Gaya bearing the footprint of Vishnu; pilgrims perform rites for their ancestors there.
[^240]: Shuklambara Brahmachari is a devotee of Navadvipa; in his house the Lord manifested prema the next day (CB Adi-khanda). Reading by sense and by CB: the 1912/13 edition (A/B) has "গরান্বয" (garānvaya), the 1960 edition "শুরাশ্বর" (śurāśvara) — both corrupted by OCR; শুক্লাম্বর (śuklāmbara) is adopted.
[^241]: Kapila is an avatara of the Lord, the son of Devahuti, who instructed his mother in knowledge and bhakti (*Bhag.*, Canto Three).
[^242]: The text has শ্রীনিবাস (Srinivasa) — here this is Srivasa, Srinivasa Pandita, not Srinivasa Acharya who is listening to the account. Cf. CB Madhya-khanda: Srivasa explained to Shachi that this was not "vayu" but bhakti-yoga.
[^243]: The king is the Muslim ruler of Gauda (Bengal); a Yavana is a foreigner of another faith, here a Muslim.
[^244]: Reading unclear (OCR): দরপ (darapa, B; A/G দরণ, daraṇa) is taken as darpa, "pride"; the sense of the line is conjectural.
[^245]: Reading unclear (OCR): before অংশুক ("garment") B has "অংশ" (aṃśa), the 1960 edition "অংগ্ত"; অঙ্গে (aṅge, "on the body") is adopted.
[^246]: Ghanashyama is the poet's signature (the second name of Narahari Chakravarti). নিছনি (nichhani) is the rite of "offering oneself" for the beloved's well-being.
[^247]: On the Lord's pastime in the mood of Varaha in the house of Murari Gupta, see CB Madhya-khanda.
[^248]: The source of the verse is not named in the text; not identified.
[^249]: "The hero Bala" is Balarama, i.e. Nityananda. Advaita exults as though it was by his call that Nityananda was brought into the world.
[^250]: The Ojha here is Hadai Pandita (Hado Ojha), Nityananda's father.
[^251]: Ghanashyama is the poetic name of Narahari Chakravarti. "Dha dha dhiki dhiki, dheya na" — dance-rhythm syllables (bols).
[^252]: According to CB (Adi-khanda), a wandering sannyasi asked the parents for the boy Nityananda as his companion, and He went away with him.
[^253]: Nandana Acharya was a devotee of Navadvipa; in his house Nityananda stayed on coming to Navadvipa (CB Madhya-khanda).
[^254]: The banner bearing a palm tree (তালধ্বজ, tāladhvaja) is the emblem of Balarama.
[^255]: "How … shines": A and B read "ঝোহে / তোহে" (jhohe / tohe), probably "সোহে" (sohe, "shines"); the 1960 ed. reads "ভুবন মোহে" (bhuvana mohe, "charms the world"). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^256]: "Offered prayers" follows the 1960 ed. "স্তুতি" (stuti); A and B read "তত" (tata), probably corrupt. Reading unclear (OCR).
[^257]: Ramai Pandita (Sri Rama Pandita, 1749) was the younger brother of Srivasa Pandita.
[^258]: "Thaita taka taka thona" — dance-rhythm syllables (bols). "Festivity" follows the 1960 ed. "বিলস" (vilasa); A and B read "খিল" (khila). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^259]: The subject is not named in the text.
[^260]: That is, ostensibly to perform the memorial rite (shraddha) for His parents at Gaya.
[^261]: In W this couplet stands without a number in the block "## 1783"; the number 1777 follows the sequence of G (between 1776 and 1778).
[^262]: Nrisimha Bhaduri was a brahmana, the father of Sita and Shri, the wives of Advaita.
[^263]: So in A and B ("যোগ্যরূপে মহা ধন্যা ধন্যা"). The 1960 ed. reads: "fit for marriage, greatly blessed in beauty and virtues" ("যোগ্য, রূপে গুণে মহা ধন্যা").
[^264]: Sita-devi (Sita Thakurani) and Shri-devi are the daughters of Nrisimha Bhaduri and the wives of Advaita Acharya. According to the *Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika* (1786), Sita is the incarnation of Yogamaya and Shri her expansion.
[^265]: Advaita Acharya called upon the Lord to descend with loud cries (CC Adi 3).
[^266]: নিজ-গৃহে — literally "in [his] own house"; possibly the house of Srivasa, to which the Lord had gone (1798), is meant.
[^267]: Pundarika Vidyanidhi is an associate of the Lord, whom the Lord called father; according to GGD he is Vrishabhanu, the father of Radha; he was the spiritual master of Gadadhara Pandita. His coming to Navadvipa and meeting with the Lord are told in CB Madhya-khanda.
[^268]: Vanga is East Bengal. Chatigrama (চাটিগ্রাম) is Chittagong (now in Bangladesh); Chakrashala (চক্রশালা) is a village near it, the birthplace of Pundarika Vidyanidhi.
[^269]: "Premanidhi" ("treasure of prema") is a play on words: "Vidyanidhi" means "treasure of learning."
[^270]: So in the 1912/13 ed.: প্রণমে ("bows"). The 1960 ed. reads প্রথমে ("first"): "Then first, recognizing the Lord as his own, he offered obeisances to Sri Advaita Acharya."
[^271]: Shachi's dream of Krishna and Balarama, the feeding of Nityananda in the Lord's house, as well as the Lord's manifestations in the forms of the avataras (1837) and the episode of the singer of songs about Shiva (1839–1846), are described in CB Madhya-khanda.
[^272]: So translated শ্যাম শুরু রূপ (A, B and G all read "শুরু"), probably শুক্ল ("white"): the dark form of Krishna and the white form of Balarama. Reading unclear (OCR).
[^273]: The Lord's absorption in the mood of Shiva is His lila.
[^274]: চরম (charama) — probably চর্ম ("hide"; the hide is Shiva's garment). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^275]: The quotation (1849–1854) is from CB Madhya-khanda.
[^276]: বলি (bali) is translated as বলী ("mighty"); "saying" is also possible. Reading unclear.
[^277]: ভাগ ভূয়শ রস (bhaga bhuyasha rasa) — the sense is unclear; translated tentatively (ভাগ = "fortune," ভূয়শঃ = "abundantly").
[^278]: সুবলনি (subalani) is tentatively translated as "slender." The 1960 ed. reads "clad in garments of Chinese silk (চীন বসন) of dense radiance"; the word চীন is absent in the 1912/13 ed. (B).
[^279]: "The former rasa" (পুরুব-রস, *puruva-rasa*) — probably the mood of Krishna's former pastimes in Vraja. "The Bodiless One" (অতনু, *atanu*) is Kamadeva.
[^280]: The raga is named আম্রপঞ্চক (*āmrapañcaka*, A/B; distorted in the OCR of the 1960 ed.). Possibly the same as the raga Amra-panchama in the list of ragas in Taranga 5. Reading unclear (OCR).
[^281]: Reading unclear (OCR): "সঙ্গীত রগিতরঙ্গিতচরণা" (*saṅgīta ragitaraṅgita-caraṇā*, B; 1960 ed. "সঙ্গীত-রঙ্গিত রর্দিত চরণ"). Translated tentatively: "feet frolicking (রঙ্গিত) in music."
[^282]: Bandhuli (বাধুলি, probably বান্ধুলি, *bāndhuli*) is the bandhuka, a bright red flower. In the 1912/13 ed. the word ওট ("lips") carries the editor's gloss "ওষ্ঠ"; not translated. Balarama Dasa is a Vaishnava poet, an author of padas; this is his signature (*bhaṇitā*).
[^283]: The "seven praharas" (সাত-প্রহরিয়া ভাব, *sāta-prahariyā bhāva*) — the Lord's great revelation in Srivasa's house, when for twenty-one hours (seven praharas of three hours each) He displayed His divine nature and sat on Vishnu's throne (CB Madhya-khanda 9).
[^284]: Following A/B (আইলা, "came"). The 1960 ed. reads হইল বিহ্বল: "[here He] became beside Himself."
[^285]: Reading unclear (OCR): "ভণ তন আয়ত" (*bhaṇa tana āyata*, B; 1960 ed. "ভনতন আয়ত"); understood as ভণত ন আয়ত — "words cannot tell it."
[^286]: Jagai and Madhai were brahmana brothers of Navadvipa who had become robbers and drunkards; the Lord and Nityananda delivered them. The command to Nityananda and Haridasa to preach from house to house (1915) and the deliverance of Jagai and Madhai are described in CB Madhya-khanda 13.
[^287]: Govinda, Madhava and Vasu (Vasudeva) Ghosha — brothers, kirtana singers, associates of Mahaprabhu.
[^288]: Cf. the account in CB (Madhya-khanda) of Srivasa's mother-in-law, who secretly hid in the house during the night kirtana.
[^289]: Cf. CB Madhya-khanda, where the Lord leaps into the Ganga and Nityananda and Haridasa pull Him out. The Lord's words about a body "devoid of prema" are His lila, displaying the mood of a devotee.
[^290]: Reading unclear (OCR): "কনক মেরু দুরে দামিনী-দাম" (*kanaka meru dure dāminī-dāma*, B; the line is distorted in the 1960 ed., and absent from A). The word দুরে is understood as "leaving far behind."
[^291]: Reading unclear (OCR): বন্ধুর (*bandhura*, B) is understood as Skt. *bandhura*, "lovely"; "শ্রুতি জিতি" (*śruti jiti*) tentatively as "in the ears … surpassing." The stanza is missing from A (in W) and from the 1960 ed. (OCR); read from B alone.
[^292]: Chandrashekhara Acharya, the Lord's uncle, was the husband of Shachi's sister. On the performance in his house, where the Lord danced in the role of Lakshmi and Sadashiva and Buddhimanta Khan prepared the costumes, see CB Madhya 18.
[^293]: The episode 1955–1983: cf. CB Madhya 19. Advaita, burdened by the Lord's reverence, deliberately extols jnana in order to receive punishment from Him.
[^294]: Shri Thakurani is apparently Shri-devi, Advaita's second wife, the sister of Sita.
[^295]: Lalitapura is a village on the way from Navadvipa to Shantipura; on the meeting of the Lord and Nityananda with the sannyasi at Lalitapura, see CB Madhya 19.
[^296]: "Branch" (শাখা): a disciple, a follower (cf. the branches of Advaita's tree in CC Adi 12). This Shankara is a disciple of Advaita who held to the doctrine of jnana; he is not mentioned under this name in CC (to be checked).
[^297]: On Shachi's offence against Advaita and its forgiveness, see CB Madhya 22.
[^298]: Narahari here is Narahari Sarakara Thakura of Shrikhanda, an associate of the Lord (not the author of the *Bhakti-ratnakara*); likewise in 2021.
[^299]: "Offer themselves": আপনা নিছয়ে (āpanā nichaye, G আপনি); the custom of nichhani, in which, out of love, something is waved around the beloved, as if offering oneself in his place.
[^300]: Srinivasa is Srivasa Pandita (Srinivasa being the full form of his name). "Showers" is বরষত (baraṣata), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition (printed with a break at the caesura, "বর-ষত").
[^301]: Vasu Ghosha is Vasudeva Ghosha (বাসু ঘোষ, *vāsu ghoṣa*, per the scan of the 1912/13 edition).
[^302]: "Changas and dhakkas": চঙ্গড়ক (caṅgaḍaka), understood as চঙ্গ + ঢক্ক (kinds of drums); "to every end": চলু ওর (calu ora); both readings are confirmed by the scan of the 1912/13 edition. "Nishanas" (large kettledrums) follows A/B নিশান (niśāna); the 1960 edition has বিষাণ (viṣāṇa, "horns"). "Cymbals" follows A ঝাঁঝ (jhāṃjha); the 1960 edition has বাজ ("sound").
[^303]: "By the former rasa" — পুরুব (*puruba*, "former"), i.e. by the mood of the former pastimes in Vraja; so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition and in the 1960 edition (the OCR of copy B, "পুরুষ", is an error).
[^304]: The lord of the gods is Indra; the Lord of Parvati is Shiva; the Four-faced is Brahma. "Humbly entreats" — করত পরিহার (*karata parihāra*): in Brajabuli পরিহার means a humble entreaty.
[^305]: The river of the gods is the Ganga. Jagannatha ("Lord of the world") here is the Deity they worship.
[^306]: The *Sri Chaitanya-charita-mahakavya* is Kavi Karnapura's Sanskrit poem (the same one called the *Sri Krishna-chaitanya-charitamrita-mahakavya* in 12.879).
[^307]: The Kazi is the Muslim magistrate and ruler of Navadvipa (Chand Kazi). On his ban of the kirtana and the Lord's nagara-kirtana see CC Adi 17; CB Madhya 23.
[^308]: "নরহরি দাস গদাধর দামোদর" is taken as "Narahari, Gadadhara Dasa, Damodara" (cf. "দাস গদাধর … নরহরি" in 12.2013); "Narahari Dasa, Gadadhara, Damodara" is also possible.
[^309]: A sampradaya here is a separate party of kirtana singers with its own lead singer.
[^310]: Ai is the Lord's mother Shachi; her daughter-in-law is Devi Vishnupriya, the Lord's wife.
[^311]: Arthavada is the view that the scriptures' glorification of the Name is mere laudatory exaggeration. This and the following episodes (bathing in His clothes, the mango tree, the clouds, "gopi, gopi") — CC Adi 17.
[^312]: So in the 1912/13 edition: মন্দিরা (*mandirā*, hand cymbals). The 1960 edition has মন্দির (*mandira*, "temple").
[^313]: Dhati (ধটী, *dhaṭī*) is a waistcloth; neta (নেত, *neta*) is a fine (silk) cloth.
[^314]: The Acharya is Advaita Acharya. Haddipa (হড্ডিপ, *haḍḍipa*) — the Hadis, a caste of sweepers and cleaners.
[^315]: Nrihari ("Hari in human form") is Mahaprabhu. "Out of compassion gave [teaching]" follows the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "… ভগবান্ স্বাত্মতন্ত্রোঽপি কারুণ্যেনাদ্য শিক্ষয়ন্" (*kāruṇyenādya śikṣayan*, "out of compassion, now teaching", a participle without a finite verb); the 1960 edition has অভ্যশিক্ষয়ৎ (*abhyaśikṣayat*, "taught"). The sense is the same.
[^316]: Keshava Bharati is the sannyasi from whom the Lord later accepted sannyasa in Kantaka-nagara (Katwa).
[^317]: "Ai" (mother, i.e. Shachi) follows the 1912/13 edition (A and B "আই"); the 1960 edition has "যাই" ("going"). On the worship of Shashthi, cf. 12.1074–1075.
[^318]: Probably the Deities of Nitai and Chaitanya served by Gauridasa Pandita at Ambika (Kalna); on them, BR 7.317–320, 356–357.
[^319]: Ananta (Shesha) is the thousand-mouthed serpent who ceaselessly sings the Lord's glories. The line may also be understood as "it would take infinite power."
[^320]: On the two thieves who carried Nimai off for His ornaments, see 12.1077–1080.
[^321]: On the tairthika brahmana, see 12.1086–1110.
[^322]: On the Ekadashi offering, see 12.1111–1115.
[^323]: On Nimai's offence toward Murari Gupta, see 12.1126–1129; in more detail, CB, Adi-khanda.
[^324]: The maidens of Nadia worshipped the gods on the bank of the Ganga, praying for good husbands; Nimai demanded their offerings (CB, Adi-khanda). The author does not name the deity meant here.
[^325]: Vallabha's daughter is Lakshmipriya, the Lord's first wife; cf. 12.1231–1238.
[^326]: Nimai's commentary (*tippani*) on grammar is also mentioned in CB, Adi-khanda.
[^327]: Krishnananda and Kamalakanta — according to CB (Adi-khanda), together with Murari Gupta, fellow students of Nimai at the school of Gangadasa Pandita. "Trick questions" — *phāṅki*: ingenious catch questions on grammar.
[^328]: For the words "শ্রীপুরীর প্রীতি" (*śrīpurīra prīti*, "Sri Puri's love") the 1912/13 edition gives a footnote variant, "মুরারির প্রীতি" (*murārira prīti*, "Murari's love").
[^329]: According to GGD, Murari Gupta is Hanuman, whom tradition regards as a portion of Rudra (Shiva).
[^330]: The *Krishna-lilamrita* is a book by Ishvara Puri; on his stay in the house of Gopinatha Acharya, how he read it to Gadadhara and asked Nimai to point out its faults, see CB Adi-khanda.
[^331]: The city of Madhu is Mathura. An allusion to how Krishna, entering Mathura, visited the weaver, the garland-maker Sudama and Kubja (*Bhag.* 10.41–42).
[^332]: The sarvajna ("all-knowing one") is a soothsayer of Navadvipa; the story of how Nimai asked him about His previous births is in CB Adi-khanda.
[^333]: Shridhara — a poor and simple-hearted devotee of Navadvipa who sold banana leaves and fruit; Nimai playfully haggled with him over the price (CB Adi-khanda).
[^334]: "Pastimes of eagerness for a fight" — *yuddha-kāma-līlā*, probably referring to the Lord's playful squabbles with Shridhara (12.2234); so also in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("যুদ্ধ কাম লীলা"), the meaning unclear. The second line follows A ("yabe," "when"); the 1960 edition has "sabe": "all do whatever the Lord wishes."
[^335]: "Srivasa" follows the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "অকস্মাৎ শ্রীবাসপণ্ডিত সনে দেখা" (the OCR readings G "pravāsa," B "ābāda" are errors).
[^336]: Keshava Kashmiri — a scholar from Kashmir, an acharya of the Nimbarka sampradaya; on the Lord's victory over this digvijayi see CC Adi 16 and CB Adi-khanda.
[^337]: There follows (2255–2271) the succession of teachers of the Nimbarka sampradaya, going back to Narayana; Hamsa is the Lord in the form of a swan; "the four Sanas" (*catuḥsana*) are the four Kumaras: Sanaka, Sanandana, Sanatana and Sanatkumara. Nimbaditya is Nimbarka.
[^338]: Srinivasacharya — an acharya of the Nimbarka sampradaya; not to be confused with Srinivasa Acharya, the hero of this book.
[^339]: The Gopala Bhatta of 2269 is a teacher of the Nimbarka sampradaya, not Gopala Bhatta Gosvami.
[^340]: The meaning of the line is unclear (the scan of the 1912/13 edition and the 1960 edition agree: বর্ণি লীলাভোগ «লঘুকেশব»-নামেতে, barṇi līlābhoga "laghukeśava"-nāmete). Probably it means that, ashamed of his former pride, Keshava Kashmiri signed his writings on the lilas with the name "Laghu-Keshava" ("the lesser Keshava").
[^341]: Tapana Mishra was a learned brahmana of East Bengal; the Lord instructed him in harinama as the path for the age of Kali and told him to settle in Kashi (CB Adi-khanda; CC Adi 16).
[^342]: While the Lord was in Bengal, His first wife, Lakshmipriya, departed from this world (cf. 12.1373–1376).
[^343]: Bengal (বঙ্গদেশ) here is East Bengal; Nimai mimicked the speech of its people (CB Adi-khanda).
[^344]: Ramai is Rama Pandita, Srivasa's younger brother. Shriman Pandita's account of the change in the Lord after Gaya and Gadadhara's weeping in Shuklambara's house — CB Madhya-khanda.
[^345]: "How the outpouring of prema began" — প্রেমারম্ভ (*premārambha*, "the beginning of prema"), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition and in the 1960 edition (the OCR of copy B, "প্রেমার্ত," is an error). For the word "ব্রহ্মচারী" ("the brahmachari") the 1912/13 edition gives a footnote variant, "গৌরহরি" ("Gaurahari").
[^346]: Ratnagarbha Acharya was a devotee of Navadvipa from the same homeland as Jagannatha Mishra; hearing him recite a verse of the *Bhagavata* about Krishna, the Lord fainted from prema (CB Madhya-khanda).
[^347]: The devotees' report to Advaita and the Lord's visit with Gadadhara to Advaita, who worships Him (2338–2347) — CB Madhya-khanda.
[^348]: Kanai Natashala is a village on the way from Bihar to Bengal (by tradition, near Rajmahal). According to CB (Madhya-khanda), on returning from Gaya the Lord told the devotees that there Krishna had appeared to Him in the form of a beautiful dark boy.
[^349]: বল্গিয়া মরয়ে (*balgiya maraye*) — according to a note in the 1912/13 edition, "leaping about and babbling in every way"; literally "they die raving." So also in 2355 (the scan of the 1912/13 edition has বল্গিয়া there too; "বলিয়া" in the OCR is an error).
[^350]: "Offer [for Him]" is nichhani (নিছয়ে); see the note on 12.979.
[^351]: A nagara is a town dandy, a gallant lover; nagarali is a nagara's finery.
[^352]: The song 2380–2383 depicts the feelings of the young women of Nadia for Gaura; see the note on 12.1404.
[^353]: "I am He" (মুই সেই), that is, Krishna, the cowherd of Vraja.
[^354]: On Srivasa's worship of Nrisimha, when the Lord, striking the door, showed him the four-armed form, see CB Madhya-khanda.
[^355]: Virasana, the "hero's posture," is a seated posture.
[^356]: Narayani was Srivasa's niece, later the mother of Vrindavana Dasa, the author of the *Chaitanya-bhagavata*.
[^357]: The quotation is from CB Madhya-khanda (Vishvambhara as Nityananda saw Him in the house of Nandana Acharya); it continues after 2429.
[^358]: "White" is শুক্ল (*śukla*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "তথি শোভে শুক্ল যজ্ঞসূত্র অতিক্ষীণ" (cf. 12.1831).
[^359]: মায়ুর (*māyura*) is the name of a raga; possibly the same as the raga Maura (মাউর) in 12.1759.
[^360]: The Vyasa-puja is the worship of Vyasa as the original guru, performed by sannyasis on the full-moon day; on Nityananda's Vyasa-puja in the house of Srivasa (2445–2467), see CB Madhya-khanda.
[^361]: বামনা (*bāmanā*) is a colloquial form of the word "brahmana."
[^362]: The sense of the second line is unclear: "নিত্যানন্দচন্দ্রে প্রকাশয়ে ভক্তি করি" (*nityānandacandre prakāśaye bhakti kari*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition; the 1960 edition has "নিত্যানন্দচান্দ্র" (*nityānandacāndra*). Also possible: "Nityanandachandra reveals [it] with reverence."
[^363]: Varuni is an intoxicating drink dear to Balarama; কাদম্বিনী (*kādambinī*), as the 1912/13 edition explains, is also "a kind of intoxicating drink" (মদবিশেষ). The Lord drank Ganga water; His mood of Balarama is lila.
[^364]: The six-armed form (ষড়্ভুজ, *ṣaḍbhuja*) of the Lord unites the features of Rama, Krishna and Gaura Himself as a sannyasi; on how Nityananda beheld it, see CB Madhya-khanda.
[^365]: Nada (নাঢ়া, *nāṛhā*) is the nickname by which the Lord called Advaita Acharya.
[^366]: Cf. 12.1745.
[^367]: Kunda is a kind of jasmine. On the inexhaustible flowering of the kunda in Srivasa's courtyard, see CB Madhya-khanda.
[^368]: Vishnu's throne (বিষ্ণুর খট্টা, *viṣṇura khaṭṭā*) is the seat of the Deity of Vishnu in the shrine of Srivasa's house; the Lord sat on it, revealing His divine nature (CB Madhya-khanda).
[^369]: Tribhanga is the pose of Krishna, bent in three places, playing the flute; Advaita sees Krishna in Gaura.
[^370]: Advaita Acharya, grieving over the fallen people of the age of Kali, worshipped Krishna with Ganga water and tulasi and called on Him to descend (CC Adi 3).
[^371]: According to the 1912/13 edition (B "বারে বারে"); the 1960 edition has "ধীরে ধীরে" — "softly, softly."
[^372]: Advaita Acharya, who lived in Shantipura, also had a house in Navadvipa (CB Adi-khanda).
[^373]: Sri Mukunda is Mukunda Datta, who, like Vidyanidhi, was a native of Chatigrama; Vasudeva Datta (2507) is his brother.
[^374]: Pandita Gosani is Gadadhara Pandita.
[^375]: On Vidyanidhi's princely outward appearance, how Gadadhara took him for a worldly man, and how this was set right through Mukunda (2511 ff.), see CB Madhya-khanda.
[^376]: The words of Uddhava to Vidura. Baki is Putana, the sister of Bakasura, who came to kill the infant Krishna with her poisoned breast.
[^377]: The verse number follows the 1960 edition and the standard numbering of the *Bhagavata*; the 1912/13 edition prints "৬।২৬" (6.26), an error.
[^378]: That is, both are the Lord's eternal associates, inseparable from Him; according to GGD Pundarika Vidyanidhi is Vrishabhanu, the father of Radha, and Gadadhara is Radha Herself.
[^379]: How the Lord tested Srivasa, asking whether he would keep Nityananda with him whatever He might do, is told in CB Madhya-khanda.
[^380]: So in B ("এই ঠাই"). The 1960 edition has "in one place" ("এক ঠাই").
[^381]: Govinda Datta is an associate of the Lord, the leading singer of His kirtana (CC Adi 10). সম্প্রদা (*sampradā*) is a group of kirtana singers with its lead singer.
[^382]: সোন (*sona*; so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition, the 1960 edition has শোন) means "gold" (সোনা). ভাঙনি (*bhāṅani*) means bearing, graceful pose.
[^383]: The Mandakini is the heavenly Ganga: tears pour from His eyes like her streams.
[^384]: Kusumashara ("He whose arrows are flowers"), Manmatha (2562), Madana (2564, 2574) and Atanu ("the Bodiless One," 2568) are names of Kamadeva, the god of love.
[^385]: "Dha dha dhigi ti" are drum-rhythm syllables (bol).
[^386]: Purandara is Indra, the king of the gods. The sense of the second half of the line is unclear: "ধনি কলি কাল ভাগ লহু পটতর" (*bhāga lahu paṭatara*, so also in the scan of the 1912/13 edition), lit. "fortune — a slight comparison."
[^387]: That is, the bhava of His former lila (as Krishna). "Former" is পুরুব (*puruba*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition (OCR: G "পুকুব," B "পুরুষ"); cf. 12.2025.
[^388]: The Kinnaras are celestial singers and musicians.
[^389]: A hastaka is a hand gesture in dance. "Swift, well-formed" follows the reading "ত্রস্ত কলিত" (*trasta kalita*; so also in the scan of the 1912/13 edition); the sense is unclear.
[^390]: In the 1912/13 edition the commas stand thus: "বামে পহু পণ্ডিত, গদাধর দক্ষিণেতে, নিতাই সুন্দর, সমুখে শ্রীঅদ্বৈত," which also allows the reading: "To the Lord's left is the Pandita, to the right Gadadhara, [beside Him] the handsome Nitai." The translation follows the metre of the stanza.
[^391]: "Rama on the left" is "রাম বামে" (*rāma bāme*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition and in the 1960 edition (B's OCR "রাস নামে" is an error). Garuda is probably Garudai (cf. 12.1852). The mardala is a drum.
[^392]: Measure: বন্ধান (*bandhāna*), the metre or structure of a musical piece in kirtana.
[^393]: "The guise of a wrestler" is মল্লবেশ (*mallaveśa*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition (OCR: G "মজবেশ," B "মন্সবেশ"). Nityananda is Balarama, famed as a wrestler.
[^394]: The lord of Shantipura is Advaita. The end of the stanza follows the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "ধরিয়া শ্রীচরণ, করয়ে রোদন, পণ্ডিত শ্রীবাস উদার" (garbled in B's OCR as "গঞ্চিত বীনান উদার"; missing from G's OCR).
[^395]: "All but die, raving" is মরয়ে বল্গিয়া (*maraye balgiyā*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition (B's OCR "বঙ্গিয়া"); cf. 12.2352.
[^396]: Murari here is a name of the Lord ("the enemy of [the demon] Mura"), not the author of the book, Murari Gupta.
[^397]: "Couch" — খট্টা (khaṭṭā). Cf. the account in CB (Madhya-khanda) of how the Lord in Srivasa's house sat on Vishnu's couch and took the shalagramas into His lap. "Sat on the couch" is "বসিলা খট্টায়," so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition and in the 1960 edition.
[^397a]: The quotation from CB (2588–2590) ends here: so the 1912/13 edition places the quotation marks.
[^398]: "In the mood of servitude" is দাস্যভাবে (*dāsya-bhāve*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition (OCR: G "দাশ্ভাবে," B "দ্যভাবে").
[^399]: Janaki is Sita; "the life of Janaki" is Rama. The Lord speaks to Murari in the mood of Rama; on Murari as Hanuman see the note on 12.2194.
[^400]: Ramadasa — "the servant of Rama."
[^401]: "In the third prakrama" — so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("তথাহি শ্রীচৈতন্যচরিতে তৃতীয়প্রক্রমে"). In the 1960 edition: "in the *Sri Krishna-chaitanya-charita*, in the second prakrama, in the seventh sarga." The *Sri Chaitanya-charita* is Murari Gupta's Sanskrit poem; the verse number is not given in the editions. The shlokas 2601–2609 continue the same quotation.
[^402]: "Now, the ashtaka" (অথাষ্টকং) is a subheading in the 1912/13 edition; in the 1960 edition an editor's heading stands in its place. The eight shlokas 2601–2608 are Murari Gupta's *Ramashtaka*. Brihaspati and Kavi (Shukra) are the planets Jupiter and Venus.
[^403]: "Like glowing gold" — অশীতকনকদ্যুতি (*aśīta-kanaka-dyuti*, "with the lustre of gold that is not cold"), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("কুর্ব্বত্যশীতকনকদ্যুতি"); the 1960 edition has অসিতকনকদ্যুতি (*asita-*). The sense of the first two lines is apparently that the five fingers of Sita, holding the lotus, look like five more petals.
[^404]: The descendant of Kushika is the sage Vishvamitra, whose sacrifice Rama protected from the rakshasas (*Ramayana*, Bala-kanda).
[^405]: A pun: "adūṣaṇa" means both "free of Dushana" (the rakshasa slain by Rama in the Dandaka forest together with Khara and Trishiras) and "free of defilement." Sugriva's enemy is Vali; the Ten-faced is Ravana.
[^406]: "Knows no restraint" — "আনন্দে আনন্দে না বাঁধে থেহ" (*nā bāṅdhe theha*, "holds no steadiness"), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition (OCR: G "না বাধে খে," B "খেহ").
[^407]: Per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "মহিম মহিমা কো কহু তুর … প্রেম অমিয় হরখি বরখি তরখিত মহি মাতিয়া" (so also B's OCR; G's OCR is garbled). "তরখিত" (*tarakhita*) is taken as "thirsting" (*tṛṣita*); "তুর" (*tura*, probably "thy," তোর) is not rendered.
[^408]: Here and further in the songs on the bank of the Ganga, the epithets about young women are motifs of nagari-bhava; see the note on 12.1404.
[^409]: The epithets about young women (2630, 2632, 2635) are motifs of nagari-bhava; see the note on 12.1404.
[^410]: The name of the raga is "বেরগুপ্ত" (*beragupta*): so printed both in the 1912/13 edition (per the scan) and in the 1960 edition; the raga is not identified (probably a corruption).
[^411]: "Adornment" — শিঙারু (*śiṅāru*, শিঙ্গার): the scan of the 1912/13 edition has "সব মদ সকল শিঙারু" (faint print; G's OCR "শিল্পার").
[^412]: "Resoundingly" — সুস্বরে (*susvare*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition (OCR: G "হম্বরে," B "স্বন্বরে").
[^413]: "Stone" — শিলা (*śilā*), so also in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("গন্ধ চন্দন শিলা ধূপ দীপ জ্বালি"); probably the stone on which sandal is ground.
[^414]: "Duhkhi" means "the wretched one," "Sukhi" — "the happy one." On Srivasa's maidservant who brought water for the Lord's abhisheka, see CB Madhya-khanda.
[^415]: Kholavecha, "seller of khola" (cups and leaves made from banana bark), is Shridhara's byname. His praise and the Lord's boon (Shridhara asked only for devotion) are in the CB account (Madhya-khanda) of the "seven praharas."
[^416]: "Murari-vallabha" (*murāri-vallabha*) — "the Beloved of Murari" (or "dear to Murari," that is, to Krishna); the text (so also the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "মুরারিবল্লভ নাম হইল প্রকাশ") does not make clear whether the name belongs to the Lord or to Murari Gupta.
[^417]: Mukunda is Mukunda Datta. The Lord at first would not admit him into His presence because he kept company with those who reject bhakti, and then forgave him (CB Madhya-khanda).
[^417a]: "গৌরাঙ্গের বাক্যে নিত্যানন্দের যে রীত" (so the 1912/13 edition): which deed is meant the text does not say; probably it looks ahead to the next couplet: at the Lord's word Nityananda stays in Srivasa's house.
[^418]: The story of the crow and Malini's bowl is in CB Madhya-khanda. The 1912/13 edition (per the scan) has no quotation marks: "শ্রীমালিনী দুঃখী দেখি জিজ্ঞাসিল তারে." Translated according to the sense of the CB account; the 1960 edition puts দুঃখী (*duḥkhī*, "distressed") in quotation marks as the maidservant's name — "Sri Malini, seeing Duhkhi, asked her."
[^419]: Chitragupta is Yama's scribe, who records the good and bad deeds of living beings.
[^420]: "The two sinners" — so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "জগাই মাধাই দুই পাপি উদ্ধারিলা" (*dui pāpi*); the 1960 edition has "দুই ভাই" (*dui bhāi*), "the two brothers."
[^421]: "Devotion" is ভক্তি (*bhakti*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("দেবের দুর্লভ ভক্তি দিলা দুই জনে") and in the 1960 edition (B's OCR "শক্তি" is an error).
[^422]: Madhai's ghat (Madhai-ghat) is a ghat on the Ganga in Navadvipa that Madhai, at Nityananda's order, built in atonement for his sins, and where he begged everyone's forgiveness (CB Madhya-khanda).
[^423]: Madhava is Madhai's name (Jagai is Jagannatha).
[^424]: "Mother-in-law" is শাশুড়ী (*śāśuḍī*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition and in the 1960 edition, as in CB (B's OCR "শালী" is an error). "Basket" is ডোল (*ḍola*); the 1912/13 edition glosses it as a covering made of rope or matting.
[^425]: Shuklambara Brahmachari lived on alms; the Lord, absorbed in kirtana, eats the raw rice from his bag, calling him Sudama — the poor brahmana, Krishna's childhood friend, whose rice Krishna lovingly ate in Dvaraka (*Bhag.* 10.80–81). The episode: CB Madhya-khanda.
[^426]: Onomatopoeia of the mridanga's beats; per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "দৃমিকি দৃমি, দাঁ দাঁ দৃমি কট, ধিকট ধিলঙ্গ" (*dṛmiki dṛmi, dāṃ dāṃ dṛmi kaṭa, dhikaṭa dhilaṅga*).
[^427]: "Glows" — লস (*lasa*): "চাঁদ বদন লস," so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition (B's OCR "লল").
[^428]: Per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "বাজত ঝাঁঝ সু-খমক খোল কত" (*jhāṃjha su-khamaka*; OCR: B "ঝাঁঝ দ্থ-খমক," 1960 ed. "বাউল খমক"). The khamak is a small percussion (plucked-string) instrument.
[^429]: "Putting to shame" — নিরসি (*nirasi*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition, with the footnote gloss "নিরসি — নিরাশ করিয়া" ("having made hopeless"); the 1960 ed. has nirakhi ("looking at"). Lapana ("face, mouth") is in both editions.
[^429a]: "প্রভু প্রিয় সঞ্জয়ের এই ঘর" (so the 1912/13 edition): apparently Mukunda Sanjaya, in whose house Nimai taught His pupils (cf. 12.1383, 2213).
[^430]: The jackfruit (panasa) is covered with small spikes — a figure for the hairs standing on end in ecstasy (pulaka).
[^431]: Per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "কনক জিনি যনু, মদন ময় তনু" (*yanu* = জনু, "as if"): "the body, as if surpassing gold, full of [the beauty of] Madana."
[^432]: Per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "সঘনে জয় জয়, ভণত অতিশয়, শ্যাম ঘন মুদ মাতিয়া" — "ভণত" ("says") introduces the poet's signature: "শ্যাম ঘন" (Shyama-ghana) is Ghanashyama, Narahari's other name (cf. 12.2559). The 1960 ed. has "হাম ঘন" ("I…").
[^433]: Here (2802) and in the songs 2803–2831, the feelings of the women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404.
[^434]: Yadunandana Chakravarti, a disciple of Dasa Gadadhara, was a poet-padakarta; his signature appears in 2807, 2814 ("Yadu"), 2823. The heading follows the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "তত্রাদৌ শ্রীদাস-গদাধরঠকুরস্য শিষ্য-শ্রীযদুনন্দনচক্রবর্ত্তি-কৃত গীতে যথা" (B's OCR "śrīvāsa-" is an error).
[^435]: "Another": কশ্চিৎ / কাচিৎ (kaścit / kācit, "a certain"; the gender agrees with the raga's name) in A/B here and at 2815, 2819, 2824, 2830; in the 1960 ed. only at 2824 and 2830. Apparently "another [song] in the raga …."
[^436]: Per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "সহজ ভাঙর ভঙ্গিমা কাজে। পরাণে আঁজুলি কি আর লাজে," understood as "the natural curving posture [so] works." Ajuli is an address to a teasing friend (after the 1912/13 edition's gloss on 2829); here it is printed আঁজুলি (*āṃjuli*), so "a handful [of farewell water]" is also possible: "[I] have given up my life — what shame is left?"
[^437]: Devakinandana is the poet whose signature stands in this song. Ajuli (আজলি, *ājali*): according to the footnote gloss of the 1912/13 edition, "one who speaks in mockery (in jest)," i.e. a teasing friend; cf. 2811.
[^438]: The songs 2832–2869 continue the theme of the feelings of the women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404. Poets' signatures: Narahari (2842, 2861), Yadunandana (2850, 2857, 2869).
[^439]: "The moon's" — ইন্দু (*indu*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("চন্দনের বিন্দু ইন্দু-গরবের কাল") and in the 1960 ed. (B's OCR "ইন্দ্র" is an error).
[^440]: কাচিচ্চ / কশ্চিচ্চ (*kācic ca / kaścic ca*, "and a certain [other]") — the word before the raga name in A/B (feminine with a ragini, masculine with a raga); rendered "And another [song]". Mallarika is the name of a ragini.
[^441]: "In every direction, joy for the women" — দিশা নারী-সুখ (*diśā nārī-sukha*), so also in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("ছটায় গগন আলো দিশা নারী সুখ"); the sense is unclear, possibly "I can find no limit to the joy" (*nāri*, "I cannot").
[^442]: The raga name: the 1960 ed. has বল্লরী (*vallarī*); the scan of the 1912/13 edition has "কাচিচ্চ বল্ললী" (*vallalī*), probably a misprint.
[^443]: The songs 2870–2879 continue the theme of the feelings of the women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404. Poets' signatures: Yadunandana (2874), Vasudeva Ghosha (2879).
[^444]: আজুলি (*ājuli*) — "simple, naive girl," an address to a friend.
[^445]: "How can family honor survive" — so in B (কুল কি রহিবে তার); the 1960 ed. has "what can family honor do to her?" (কুল কি করিবে তার).
[^446]: Shadja is the first of the seven svaras; by tradition the peacock's cry resembles this note.
[^447]: Ghanashyama is the poetic name of Narahari Chakravarti. "So wretched" — দীন (*dīna*), so also in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("ভণত ঘন ঘন-শ্যাম ঐছন, দীন কি হোয়ব আর").
[^448]: "পুনশ্চ গৌরড়ী" (*gauraḍī*) — so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition and in the 1960 ed.; taken as raga Gauri (cf. 12.2018).
[^449]: "Crest-jewels of the accomplished" — so in B (সুঘর-শিরোমণি); the 1960 ed. has "crest-jewels of skilled dancers" (সুনট-শিরোমণি).
[^450]: According to CB (Madhya 18), the Lord allowed only those who had conquered their senses to watch this dance; then Advaita and Srivasa began to refuse to go, and the Lord by His will made everyone "yogeshvaras," masters of yoga, beyond agitation. In this dance the Lord appeared in the role of Rukmini.
[^451]: Per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "কর্ণভূষণ, তরল মৃদুতর, গণ্ড যুগ যনু, ভ্রমর ভুরু বর" (*yanu* = জনু, "as if"); the 1960 edition has ভ্রমণ (*bhramaṇa*, "swinging") for ভ্রমর. "The hair charmingly bound" takes বন্ধন ("binding"); the print has "চিকুর চয় কমনীয় বন্দন."
[^452]: Per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "কঞ্চু অঞ্চিত বক্ষ মৃদুতর" (*kañcu* = কঞ্চুক, "bodice") and "কনকাঙ্গুলি-অঙ্গুরী জনু রঞ্জনা" (*janu rañjanā*, "as if for delight"); the OCR of both copies is garbled here.
[^453]: "Pouring forth rasa" — রস ঝরু (*rasa jharu*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("অতুল উদর, সুঠান রস ঝরু") and in the 1960 edition (B's OCR "রস বর"). "Offers [his] life [for Him]" is nichhani (*nichhani*); see the note to 12.979. Narahari is the poet's signature.
[^454]: Cf. CB Madhya 18: in the house of Chandrashekhara Acharya the Lord, in the mood of the Mother of the world, gave the devotees His breast; the same chapter tells of the effulgence that remained in his house for seven days (2929).
[^455]: Not in the 1960 edition.
[^456]: The episode of Murari Gupta's bowing and his dream (2935–2953) — CB Madhya 20.
[^457]: "Today go home" follows B অদ্য গৃহে যা[ও] (*adya gṛhe yā[o]*); the 1960 edition has অন্য গৃহে যাই (*anya gṛhe yāi*, "I shall go to another house").
[^458]: Prakashananda [Sarasvati] — a Mayavada sannyasi of Kashi (Varanasi) who taught that the Lord's form is unreal; later Mahaprabhu converted him in Varanasi (CC Adi 7, Madhya 25).
[^459]: Cf. CB Madhya 20: in ecstasy Murari offered Krishna rice with ghee by handfuls, saying "Eat!" — hence the Lord's "indigestion" (2957–2962).
[^460]: Cf. CB Madhya 20: Murari resolved to leave the body before the Lord's disappearance, and the Lord, coming to him, took the knife away.
[^461]: Visharada's embankment (*jangala*) — জাঙ্গাল (*jāṅgāla*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("এই বিশারদের জাঙ্গাল এই খানে") and in the 1960 edition (B's OCR "জল"). Devananda Pandita — a reciter of the *Bhagavata* in Navadvipa who offended Srivasa; the meeting with him and with the drunkards (2976–2980) — CB Madhya 21.
[^462]: Maheshvara Visharada — the father of Vasudeva Sarvabhauma, the famous logician who lived in Puri (Nilachala) at the court of King Prataparudra; Gopinatha Acharya was his sister's husband (CC Madhya 6).
[^463]: The sense is unclear: "তেঁহ গেলে যে যে ভক্ত প্রভুরে মিলিল। সে সভে না দেখে তাঁর মনে খেদ হৈল" (so the 1912/13 edition; *se sabhe nā dekhe tāṃra mane kheda haila*) — it can also be understood as "those [devotees] did not see him, and there was sorrow in his heart."
[^464]: Narahari — Narahari Sarakara, the Lord's associate, not the author of the poem.
[^465]: Yadunatha Dasa is a Vaishnava poet, an author of padas; this is his signature (bhanita).
[^466]: Govinda Ghosha is an associate of the Lord, a kirtana singer, brother of Madhava and Vasudeva Ghosha; this is his signature (bhanita).
[^467]: Shuka is Shukadeva, the narrator of the *Bhagavata*; Virinchi is Brahma. Kamala (3002) is Lakshmi.
[^468]: The poet's signature (bhanita); which Ramachandra is meant, the text does not say.
[^469]: "চাঁচর চিকুর চূড়া ভালে সে বলিয়া" (*cūṛā bhāle se baliyā*) — so also in the scan of the 1912/13 edition; the sense is not quite clear, translated by sense — "gathered in a topknot above the forehead."
[^470]: Shekhara Raya (Raya-shekhara) is a Vaishnava poet, an author of padas in Brajabuli; this is his signature (bhanita).
[^471]: Srinivasa here (and in 3029) is Srivasa Pandita, whose full name is Srinivasa, not Srinivasa Acharya.
[^472]: Shamana is Yama, the lord of death. The heavenly trees are the wish-fulfilling trees (surataru): the Lord surpassed them in generosity.
[^473]: "The earth" follows B ("ধরণি"); the 1960 edition has "তরুণী" ("young women"), probably an OCR error.
[^474]: The 1912/13 edition notes a variant reading: "Gokulapati" ("Lord of Gokula") instead of "Golokapati."
[^475]: Shripada is the Lord's respectful way of addressing Nityananda.
[^476]: Ananga ("the bodiless one") is Kama, the god of love; likewise in 3071.
[^477]: "ত্রিভুবন দরপিত এ দোঁহার রসে" — so also in the scan of the 1912/13 edition; দরপিত (*darapita*) is understood as দরবিত ("melted"; cf. on the same page "না দরপে … পাষাণ হৃদয়," "the heart of stone does not melt"). "These two" are Gaura and Gadadhara.
[^478]: Svarupa is Svarupa Damodara. Yadu is the poet's signature (bhanita), a short form of the name (Yadunatha or Yadunandana); likewise in 3053 and 3060.
[^479]: Aja is Brahma, Bhava is Shiva.
[^480]: The Thief (চোরা) — probably Krishna, the "stealer" of butter and of hearts, hidden in Gaura's golden form.
[^481]: The Sanskrit note (শ্রীনরহরিসরকারঠক্কুরস্য গীতমিদম্) stands after this song in the 1912/13 and 1960 editions: the Narahari Dasa of the signature is Narahari Sarakara, not the author of the *Bhakti-ratnakara*. The Pandita is Gadadhara Pandita: Radha stands at Krishna's left.
[^482]: Kanu is Krishna. The comparisons refer to Gaura and Gadadhara. Rati is the consort of Kama.
[^483]: Nayanananda was a Vaishnava poet, an author of padas, the nephew and disciple of Gadadhara Pandita; this is his signature (bhanita). The note "this is a song of Sri Narahari Sarakara Thakura," which stands before this song, in the scan of the 1912/13 edition closes the preceding song (3063–3067, signed Narahari Dasa) and does not belong to the song 3068–3075.
[^484]: Per the scan of the 1912/13 edition: "সুরধুনীতীরে নাচে রঙ্গিয়া সঙ্গিয়া" (*raṅgiyā saṅgiyā*, "merry, with the companions"); the OCR of both copies is garbled.
[^485]: Srinivasa here is Srivasa Pandita (Srivasa is a short form of the name Srinivasa), not Srinivasa Acharya.
[^486]: Yadu is the poet's signature (bhanita).
[^487]: Govinda is probably Govinda Ghosha, the brother of Vasudeva Ghosha, a kirtana singer. "His qualities" renders নিজ-গুণ (*nija-guṇa*, "own qualities").
[^488]: Phagu is the red powder that people throw on one another at the Holi festival.
[^489]: চাঁদ-চন্দন (*cā̃da-candana*) is understood as sandal with camphor (চাঁদ, camphor); the Lord's golden body is compared to the golden mountain Sumeru.
[^490]: "His own ghat" renders নিজ-ঘাট (*nija-ghāṭa*), the Lord's ghat. Madhai's ghat is a ghat on the Ganga which, according to CB (Madhya-khanda), the repentant Madhai made. Madhai's ghat, Barakona-ghat (3125) and Nagariya-ghat (3126) are ghats of the Ganga in Navadvipa on the route of the nagara-kirtana.
[^491]: Kshetrapala means the guardian of the holy land; here, Shiva as the guardian of Navadvipa.
[^492]: মূর্তি (*mūrti*) is understood as the Deity of Ganesha, which disappeared when the Lord took sannyasa (3129); it may also be understood as the "form" of Ganesha himself.
[^493]: "Weep" — কাঁদে (*kā̃de*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("কাঁদে নিত্যানন্দাদ্বৈত-আদি যত জন") and in the 1960 edition; "কাছে" in the OCR of copy B is an error.
[^494]: Patadanga — "পাটডাঙ্গা" (*pāṭaḍāṅgā*), so in the scan of the 1912/13 edition ("গাদিগাছা পাটডাঙ্গা-আদি গ্রাম দিয়া"); the 1960 edition has "পারডাঙ্গা" (*pāraḍāṅgā*, Paradanga). On Gadigachha see 12.239.
[^495]: CB Madhya 23; the 1960 edition gives 23.513. The quotation is in Bengali verse from the *Chaitanya-bhagavata*.
[^496]: On the morning after Janmashtami (3148–3152) the Nanda-utsava is celebrated, Nanda's festival for the birth of his son, when the cowherds of Gokula joyfully drenched one another with yogurt, milk, ghee and turmeric (cf. *Bhag.* 10.5).
[^497]: Sundarananda and Gauridasa Pandita are associates of Nityananda; in Krishna's lila they are, according to GGD, cowherd boys (among the twelve gopalas). "Sundara" in 3163 and 3170 is Sundarananda.
[^498]: Carrying-pole renders ভার (*bhāra*): a pole on the shoulder with loads hung at both ends (here, pots).
[^499]: Reading unclear (OCR): B স্থঅট মিভাদর (G lost); read as সু-অটমি ভাদর (*su-aṭami bhādara*), "the blessed ashtami of [the month of] Bhadra," Krishna's birthday.
[^500]: Reading unclear (OCR): B নট পটি পাগ স্ুভর (*naṭa paṭi pāga subhara*), G নটপটা পাগ মৃদুভৱর (*naṭapaṭā pāga mṛdubhara*); পাগ is a turban, the sense of the other words is conjectural.
[^501]: The ascetic renders যতি (*yati*); apparently Nityananda the avadhuta.
[^502]: "Dha dhi dhi ki taka…" are syllables of the drum rhythm (bols); likewise in 3195.
[^503]: Radha's birth festival (Radhashtami) falls on the bright ashtami of Bhadra, half a month after Janmashtami. Pundarika Vidyanidhi, according to GGD, is Vrishabhanu, Radha's father; hence the festival is held in his house (cf. 3196).
[^504]: Gadadhara Pandita, according to GGD, is Sri Radha.
[^505]: Kama (কাম, *kāma*) is Kamadeva, the god of love. The 1960 edition has গোপরূপের ("of the cowherd's form") instead of গোরারূপের ("of Gora's form"). The 1912/13 edition notes for কনকের কাম the variant reading কনকের দাম ("a golden garland").
[^506]: Ketaki (ketaka) is the screw pine with fragrant golden-yellow flowers; gorochana is a bright yellow fragrant pigment obtained from cows.
[^507]: The song 3204–3209, like the song of the dana-lila (3220–3223), touches on the feelings of the women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404.
[^508]: Sangali (সাঙলি, *sāṅali*) and Dhavali (ধবলি, *dhavali*, "White") are names of cows. In G the line is corrupt (শালি ধ্লি' বি); the reading follows B.
[^509]: The dana-lila is Krishna's pastime of demanding a "toll" (দান, *dāna*) from the gopis on their way; on the song 3220–3223 see the note on 12.1404.
[^510]: Reading unclear (OCR): B and G have ফুলবল (*phulabala*); read as ফুলবন (*phulabana*), "flower garden."
[^511]: Pasha-sari (পাশা-সারি, *pāśā-sāri*) is a game of dice played with pieces on a board.
[^512]: Reading unclear (OCR): B গৌরাগ্গট দের, G lost (নেলচন); read as গৌরাঙ্গচাঁদের (*gaurāṅgacā̃dera*).
[^513]: *Duyā*, *chāri*, *pañcha*, *tina* — "two," "four," "five," "three": the scores that players call out as they throw the dice in the game of pasha-sari.
[^514]: Here and in the song 3271–3274, the feelings of the women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404.
[^515]: Reading unclear (OCR): B মৃদু মৃদু ৬ম হানিয়া (*mṛdu mṛdu … hāniyā*); the 1960 edition's text for 3271–3273 is missing from the OCR copy. Translated as মৃদু মৃদু হাস হাসিয়া, "smiling gently, gently."
[^516]: The 1960 edition reads গোরারূপ মেঘের বিজুরী, "Gora's form is lightning in a cloud."
[^517]: Translated according to B: ভুঞ্জিব কি সভারে ভুঞ্জায়. The 1960 edition reads ভুঞ্জিব কি? — সভারে সুধায়, "'Shall I eat?' He asks everyone."
[^518]: Phagu is a colored (usually red) powder that people throw on one another at the spring festival of Holi.
[^519]: Abir and gulal are coloured (red) powders that people throw on one another at Holi; a pichkari (3315, 3322) is a syringe-pump from which coloured water is squirted.
[^520]: A motif of the feelings of the young women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404.
[^521]: The text has শ্রীনিবাস (Srinivasa) — here Srivasa (Srinivasa Pandita), not Srinivasa Acharya, who is listening to the account.
[^522]: Narahari, here and in 3345, is Narahari Sarakara, the Lord's associate, not the author of the poem. "Hari" (হরি) is probably a shortened name of one of the associates (Haridasa?); the text does not make it clear.
[^523]: Reading unclear (OCR): B has "ছিল হরিদাস" (*chhila haridāsa*; in the 1960 edition lines 3330–3334 were lost in recognition); probably দ্বিজ হরিদাস (*dvija haridāsa*, "Haridasa the brahmana").
[^524]: "Thukkuta thai…", "dradrimi ki…" are syllables of the dance rhythm (bols) and the sounds of the drums; the OCR reading is approximate. The muraja is a kind of drum.
[^525]: Gadadhara Pandita, according to GGD, is Radha; hence "a woman full of rasa". The "Pandita" in 3346 is the same.
[^526]: Shivananda is the signature of the song's author (probably Shivananda Sena, the Lord's associate).
[^527]: Balarama performed the rasa-lila with the gopis in Vraja (*Bhag.* 10.65); Nityananda is Balarama, hence "Nityananda-Haladhara".
[^528]: The "former pastime" is Krishna's stealing of the gopis' garments (*Bhag.* 10.22).
[^529]: The *Sri Chaitanya-charita* is the Sanskrit biography of the Lord composed by Murari Gupta (see 12.1143); the numbers of the prakrama and sarga are given in the text of the editions. Both verses (3356–3357) come under one introductory line.
[^530]: Murari (lit. "the enemy of [the demon] Mura") is here a name of the Lord; so the 1960 edition understands it.
[^531]: CC Madhya 19 (the chapter on the instructions to Sri Rupa; the 1960 edition also gives a reference to it); Narahari calls the Madhya-lila the "Madhya-khanda".
[^532]: The five verses (3366–3370) come under one introductory line; the source is not named and has not been identified. These are the eight periods of the day (ashtakaliya-lila) of Gaura: the end of night, morning, forenoon, midday, afternoon, evening, the beginning of night and night; the verb "remember" is implied in the verses (so in the 1960 edition).
[^533]: Reading unclear (OCR): the beginning of the line in B is "পুর্ববাহ্রমদ্্রে" (*purvāhramadre*), garbled in the 1960 edition; following the 1960 edition's translation ("very eager to go to the devotees' houses") it is taken as *pūrvāhne gamane*.
[^534]: The 1960 edition has "raga Shri".
[^535]: "For a moment" — পল ছন (*pala chhana*), taken as *pala-kṣaṇa*; reading unclear (OCR).
[^536]: Gopala Chapala — a brahmana of Navadvipa, an opponent of kirtana. His offence against Srivasa, his leprosy and cure, and also the curse of the brahmana who was not admitted to the kirtana (3410–3415), are described in CC Adi 17; according to the CC, Gopala Chapala was cured when, at the Lord's word, he begged Srivasa's forgiveness.
[^537]: The Lord rejoiced at the curse "may Your worldly happiness be destroyed," for He Himself intended to leave worldly life and accept sannyasa (CC Adi 17).
[^538]: Chaitanyadasa — a poet, the author of this song (his signature).
[^539]: Vasu Ramananda (Ramananda Vasu) — a poet, the author of this song (his signature). The chakora is a bird which, according to poetic tradition, feeds on moonlight.
[^540]: Advaita's dance in the mood of a gopi, the revelation of the universal form to Him and Nityananda's arrival (3439–3453) are described in CB Madhya 24.
[^541]: It seems that Advaita is the speaker in the verse ("those bound to Me" are His followers, who worship the Lord in different rasas and in different avataras): He asks for the boon of turning them all towards Vrindavana.
[^542]: In the CB Nityananda and Advaita often playfully scold each other; this is Their lila, not enmity.
[^543]: The departure of Srivasa's son during the kirtana, and the teaching that the Lord spoke through the mouth of the dead boy, are described in CB Madhya 25.
[^544]: Sri Vijaya (Vijaya Dasa) was the Lord's scribe, who copied books for Him; in CC Adi 10 the Lord calls him Ratna-bahu. His vision from the touch of the Lord's hand in Shuklambara's house is described in CB (Madhya-khanda).
[^545]: মধু (*madhu*) means both "honey" and an intoxicating drink (varuni) dear to Balarama. In the mood of Balarama (Haladhara) the Lord demands it, and Nityananda gives Him Ganga water.
[^546]: Devakinandana is the poet; this is his signature (bhanita); see 12.2829. "A rogue, a boar wallowing in the mire" renders ভাড়িয়া গড়িয়া শূকর (*bhāṛiyā gaṛiyā śūkara*, so in the 1960 edition and in B), understood as ভাঁড়িয়া ("rogue") and গড়িয়া ("wallowing"). The reading is unclear (OCR).
[^547]: Balarama is the signature of the poet Balarama Dasa (bhanita).
[^548]: "In bewilderment" renders ধাঁদে (*dhā̃de*; ধাদে in the 1960 edition and in B), understood as "in confusion." The reading is unclear (OCR).
[^549]: Yadu is the poet's signature (bhanita).
[^550]: The students offended the Lord when, in ecstasy, He was chanting "gopi, gopi," and He chased them with a stick; after this He resolved to accept sannyasa in order to deliver the offenders as well. CC Adi 17; CB Madhya-khanda.
[^551]: CB Madhya-khanda; the 1960 edition gives 27.13–14. The quotation (3511–3512) is in Bengali verse from the *Chaitanya-bhagavata*.
[^552]: "As much as [you] could" follows the sense; B has যত্ত পায় (*yatta pāy*), the 1960 edition যত পানয় (*yata pānaya*). The reading is unclear (OCR).
[^553]: CB Madhya-khanda; no verse number is given in the editions. The quotation (3519–3520) is in Bengali verse from the *Chaitanya-bhagavata*.
[^554]: "The flowers do not bloom" follows the sense, reading না বিকসে (*nā vikase*); the 1960 edition and B have নাবিক সে (*nābika se*). The reading is unclear (OCR).
[^555]: A Sanskrit drama by Kavi Karnapura; the verse number is not given in the editions. The chintamani is the wish-fulfilling gem. "The ten directions": B has দিশো দশো (*diśo daśo*), G দিশো দশা — understood according to the gloss of the 1960 edition (দশ দিশঃ, "the ten directions").
[^556]: CC Madhya 3 (in CC, the Madhya-lila). The quotation is in Bengali verse from the *Chaitanya-charitamrita*.
[^557]: Phuliya follows B, ফুলিয়া (*phuliyā*); the 1960 edition has কুলিয়া (*kuliyā*, Kuliya). Phuliya is a village on the Ganga near Shantipura where Haridasa Thakura lived. Reading unclear (OCR: ফ/ক).
[^558]: "To Phuliya" follows B, ফুলিয়া; the 1960 edition has কুলিয়া (Kuliya) here too. "By way of Kuliya" — কুলিয়া হইয়া in all copies; on Kuliya see 12.365. Reading unclear (OCR: ফ/ক).
[^559]: Bhiksha is the meal offered to a sannyasi.
[^560]: "Of the arms" follows B, বাহাশোভা (understood as বাহু-শোভা, *bāhu-śobhā*); the 1960 edition has বহু শোভা (*bahu śobhā*), "what great beauty." Reading unclear (OCR).
[^561]: "… ঘন, শ্যামের হৃদয়-মাঝে" (*ghana, śyāmera hṛdaya-mājhe*) is understood as the signature of Ghanashyama (the other name of Narahari Chakravarti); it may also be read "shine constantly in the heart of Shyama."
[^562]: CB Antya-khanda. The quotation is in Bengali verse from the *Chaitanya-bhagavata*.
[^563]: "To take upon oneself the ills (balai)" — an expression of tender love: "may all the ills that threaten you fall on me."
[^564]: CB Antya 5; the 1960 edition gives 5.231–233. Ojha, "teacher," is an honorific byname of Raghunatha Vaidya.
[^565]: "Here comes" renders আয়ত (*āyata*), understood as Brajabuli "comes, goes." The avadhuta is Nityananda.
[^566]: Cf. CC Adi 5: Sankarshana, Shesha and the purusha-avataras are expansions of Balarama (Nityananda). "Narayana the source of the avataras" (অবতারী নারায়ণ) here apparently means Maha-Vishnu, the purusha-avatara. B has শেপশারী সন্কীর্ভন — corrupted by OCR; following G, শেষশায়ী সঙ্কর্ষণ.
[^567]: "Swells" follows the 1960 ed., স্ফীত (*sphīta*); B has শীত (*śīta*, "grows cold"). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^568]: Yadu is the poet's signature (bhanita). The poet laments that he alone was left unsubmerged by the flood of prema. The end of the line is রহল অবধি (*rahala avadhi*, "remained aside / beyond the limit"; B অনধি); in the 1960 ed. the name reads যহু (*yahu*). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^569]: "Enclosed" follows the 1960 ed., আবদ্ধ (*ābaddha*); B has অবধি (*avadhi*, "limit"). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^570]: Panihati is a village on the bank of the Ganga north of Calcutta, the home of Raghava Pandita; here Nityananda, having come from Puri, began His preaching in Bengal (CB Antya 5).
[^571]: Raghava Pandita, the Lord's associate from Panihati, is famous for the bags (jhali) of delicacies he brought to the Lord in Puri every year; Makaradhvaja Kara was the keeper of those bags (CC Antya 10).
[^572]: The motif of chaste women's feelings for the Lord, as in the songs of nagari-bhava; see the note on 12.1404.
[^573]: Sri Raghavananda is apparently Raghava Pandita himself (cf. 3663–3665).
[^574]: CB Antya 5; the 1960 ed. gives 5.303. The quotation is Bengali verse of the *Chaitanya-bhagavata*.
[^575]: CB Antya 5; the 1960 ed. gives 5.418–419 (the figures partly corrupted by OCR). "Servants" (3677) follows B, ভৃত্যগণেরে (*bhṛtyagaṇere*); the 1960 ed. has বিপ্রগণেরে (*vipragaṇere*), "brahmanas." "Desired" (3676): B বাঞ্ছিত (*vāñchita*), the 1960 ed. অভীষ্ট (*abhīṣṭa*); the meaning is the same.
[^576]: Sri Chaitanyadasa and Murari Pandita are associates of Nityananda; in B the names stand without a comma ("চৈতন্যদাস মুরারি পণ্ডিত"), so a single person is also possible — Murari Chaitanyadasa (see 9.359–401). Reading unclear.
[^577]: "Granted the meeting" follows B, মিলায়ল (*milāyala*, "brought together, united"); the 1960 ed. has বিলায়ল (*bilāyala*), "distributed." Reading unclear (OCR).
[^578]: Saptagrama is an ancient trading town on the Ganga (Hugli), north of Khardaha; there lived Uddharana Datta, an intimate associate of Nityananda from the merchant caste (the suvarna-baniks; cf. 3751).
[^579]: "The former pastimes" are the pastimes of Krishna in Vraja, where Nityananda was Balarama. The line is understood tentatively: পুরুব বিলাসী রঙ্গী সঙ্গের সঙ্গিয়া (*puruba bilāsī raṅgī saṅgera saṅgiyā*).
[^580]: Prasada Dasa is the poet's signature: in the 1960 edition "পরসাদ দাস" (*parasāda dāsa*), in B "পরমাদ দাস" (*paramāda dāsa*). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^581]: "A jewelled necklace sways" follows B "তরল মণিহার" (*tarala maṇihāra*); the 1960 edition has "সুললিত হার" (*sulalita hāra*), "a lovely necklace."
[^582]: লক্ষে লক্ষে (*lakṣe lakṣe*) is understood as "with leaps" (cf. লম্ফ); it could also be understood as "by hundreds of thousands," i.e. with countless crowds.
[^583]: Ramai is probably Ramadasa (Abhirama), Sundara is Sundarananda, and Gauridasa is Gauridasa Pandita — all three associates of Nityananda. The reading "beautiful Ramai" is also possible.
[^584]: Jnanadasa is a sixteenth-century Vaishnava poet, author of padas, a follower of Nityananda and Jahnava; this is his signature.
[^585]: Nagara, "the beloved, the gallant hero," is an epithet of Gaura in songs about the feelings of the women of Nadia for Gaura (nagari-bhava); see the note on 12.1404.
[^586]: Lochana (Lochana Dasa) is a Vaishnava poet, the author of the *Chaitanya-mangala*; this is his signature.
[^587]: Reading unclear (OCR): the end of the first line is "বরলীলা ধাত" (*baralīlā dhāta*) in B, "বরলীলা যতি" (*baralīlā yati*) in the 1960 edition; the end of the second line is "পুলকিত গাত" (*pulakita gāta*, "body covered with thrills") in B, "পুলকিত গতি" (*pulakita gati*) in the 1960 edition. Translated after B; ধাত is understood tentatively (as "receptacle, treasury").
[^588]: Not in the 1960 edition: couplets 3781a–3781c (with Ghanashyama's signature) are found only in B.
[^589]: "In that mood" — so B, সেভাবে (*se bhāve*); the 1960 edition has সে ভয়ে (*se bhaye*, "in that fear").
[^590]: "Slaps His arms like a wrestler" — মালসাট (*mālasāṭ*), a wrestler's challenge. Kali is the personified age of Kali; B's কপি (*kapi*) is an OCR error.
[^591]: "His fame" — ও না যশে (*o nā yaśe*) is taken as "with his fame." Reading unclear (OCR).
[^592]: "Slapping [His palm] under His arm" — কাখতালি (*kā̃khatāli*): striking the palm pressed under the armpit, a gesture of jubilation.
[^593]: Ghanashyama is the poet's signature (the second name of Narahari Chakravarti), not Krishna.
[^594]: The CB (Antya-khanda) tells of Nityananda's coming from Saptagrama to Advaita in Shantipura and then to Navadvipa.
[^595]: Vasu — B has বানু (*bānu*), probably a corruption of বাসু (*vāsu*): Vasu Ghosha, the author of the song, in the signature (bhanita). The 1960 edition has সেই (*sei*): "why does she not die." Reading unclear (OCR).
[^596]: "To Srivasa's house" — so B, শ্রীবাসভবনে (*śrīvāsa-bhavane*); the 1960 edition has প্রবাস-ভবনে (*pravāsa-bhavane*, "to a temporary lodging"), probably an OCR error; likewise in 3842 (G মালিনী-প্রবাসে for মালিনী শ্রীবাসে).
[^597]: The story of the robbers who plotted to steal Nityananda's ornaments and of their conversion — CB Antya-khanda.
[^598]: The quotation (3847–3850) is in Bengali verse from the *Chaitanya-bhagavata*. In the 1960 edition 3847 begins with এই মত ("thus").
[^599]: Khanayora, Baragachhi, Dogachhiya — villages where Nityananda wandered. Khanayora is the probable reading খানাযোড়া (*khānāyoṛā*); B has খালাযোড়া (*khālāyoṛā*), the 1960 edition খানচৌড়া (*khānacauṛā*). Reading unclear (OCR). On Kuliya see 12.372.
[^600]: By His loud calls (roars) Advaita induced the Lord to descend (CC Adi 3).
[^601]: The father of Achyuta is Advaita, the father of Achyutananda.
[^602]: Saranga — the 1960 edition has সারগ (probably সারঙ্গ, *sāraṅga*); B has মারঙ্গ (*māraṅga*). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^603]: Hari Hod was the local ruler (in 3933, "raja") of the village of Baragachhi (on it see 12.3848–3850); his son Krishnadasa Hod was an associate of Nityananda.
[^604]: Suryadasa Sarakhel is the elder brother of Gauridasa Pandita (cf. BR 7.330). "Sarakhel" (সরখেল, *sarakhela*) is a title conferred by the Muslim ruler of Gauda, from Persian *sar-i-khail*, "head of a troop."
[^605]: The *Vaishnavabhidhana* is apparently a Sanskrit enumeration of Vaishnavas composed by Devakinandana Dasa, the author of the *Vaishnava-vandana* (see the note on 12.94).
[^606]: Gai: see the note on 11.441. Karana (করণ, *karaṇa*) here apparently means the marriage connections of a kulina family, by which its purity was judged.
[^607]: Kulinas are the highest rank of Bengali brahmanas. The sense: the family from which Hadai Pandita took his wife (Padmavati) is also related to kulinas; literally "surrounded by kulinas" (কুলীনে বেষ্টিত, *kulīne beṣṭita*); cf. the note on 11.441.
[^608]: Varuni and Revati are the consorts of Balarama; the GGD identifies Vasudha and Jahnava with them. "Vasu" (বসু) is a short form of the name Vasudha. B has বসন (*basana*) for বসু — an OCR error.
[^609]: "A seat" — after the 1960 edition: আসনে (*āsane*); B has আপনে (*āpane*, "himself"), probably an OCR error. Reading unclear (OCR).
[^610]: "Undertaken" — after B: করিয়াছ (*kariyācha*, "have done"); the 1960 edition has কহিয়াছ (*kahiyācha*) — "what you have said." Reading unclear (OCR).
[^611]: After B: সভার উল্লাস (*sabhāra ullāsa*) — "everyone rejoiced"; the 1960 edition has পরম উল্লাস (*parama ullāsa*) — "there was supreme joy."
[^612]: After B: নিত্যানন্দে সভে লৈয়া (*nityānande sabhe laiyā*) — "everyone, taking Nityananda along"; the 1960 edition has নিত্যানন্দ সভে লৈয়া — "Nityananda, taking everyone along."
[^613]: This Krishnadasa is Suryadasa's younger brother (Krishnadasa Sarakhel, CC Adi 11), not Krishnadasa the son of Hari Hod of Baragachhi (3872).
[^614]: Krishnadasa Pandita is Suryadasa's younger brother, whom Suryadasa sent on ahead with the articles for the rite (12.3940–3941); not to be confused with Krishnadasa, son of Raja Hari Hor, Nityananda's host in Baragachhi (12.3933, 3938).
[^615]: "As if" takes the reading জনু (*janu*); the 1960 edition has "যনু," B "মনু." Reading unclear (OCR). Ketaki is the pandanus, a flower with golden petals.
[^616]: "Cannot keep her feet on the ground" is literal. The second half (হিয়া ধৈরয বাঁধে, *hiyā dhairaya bā̃dhe*, "binds the heart with patience") is understood as "[barely] holds her heart"; the reading in B is corrupt.
[^617]: "The most excellent bridegroom" takes the reading সুবর (*subara*); the 1960 edition has "স্থবর," B "স্রথর" — possibly সুন্দর (*sundara*, "beautiful"). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^618]: "The brahmanas" takes the reading ভূসুর (*bhūsura*, "gods on earth"); the 1960 edition has "ভূম্গুর," B "ভূঙ্র"; possibly ভৃঙ্গ (*bhṛṅga*, "bees," intoxicated by His beauty as by honey). Reading unclear (OCR).
[^619]: "Taka-taka thai-ta…," "thonga na-na-na…" are dance-rhythm syllables and drum bols; the OCR reading is approximate. A tana is a melodic run, an ornament of the melody.
[^620]: "All the people" follows B, সব লোক (*saba loka*); the 1960 edition has সপ্তলোক (*saptaloka*): "the path along which the seven worlds move is resplendent."
[^621]: Varuni and Revati are the consorts of Balarama; Nityananda is Balarama (Haladhara) in Gaura's lila.
[^622]: A quotation from Kavi Karnapura's *Gaura-ganoddesha-dipika*. "From portions" follows the 1960 edition, অংশসম্ভবে (*aṁśa-sambhave*); B has "বংশসম্ভবে" (*vaṁśa-sambhave*, "born of the line"), probably an OCR error. Reading unclear (OCR). Kakudmi (Raivata) is the king, Revati's father, who gave her in marriage to Balarama (*Bhag.* 9.3).
[^623]: Kalavani follows the 1960 edition, কলাবাণী (*kalābāṇī*); B has "কালাবাণী." Ananga Manjari is, in the Vraja lila, the younger sister of Sri Radha. "By the earlier principle" (পূর্বন্যায়াৎ, *pūrva-nyāyāt*) apparently refers to what the GGD says above: several persons can be combined in one associate of the Lord.
[^624]: Literally, "offer tilanjali to [their] families" — the funeral libation of water with sesame; "to give tilanjali" means to part with something forever. A poetic figure for Nityananda's irresistible beauty.
[^625]: Kshetra is Sri Kshetra (Puri). How Nityananda broke Mahaprabhu's sannyasa staff on the way to Puri is told in CC Madhya 5 and CB Antya 2.
[^626]: The son of Nabha is Advaita Acharya, the son of Nabha Devi and Kuvera Pandita.
[^627]: Lakshmi is Lakshmipriya, the Lord's first wife; Vishnupriya is His second wife.
[^628]: Mukunda in 4054 is probably Mukunda Datta, the singer of the Lord's kirtana; Sri Mukunda here is Mukunda Dasa of Shrikhanda, the elder brother of Narahari Sarakara and the father of Raghunandana.
[^629]: Srivasa has already been named in 4053; so in the 1912/13 edition (B: শ্রীবাসপণ্ডিত, *śrīvāsa-paṇḍita*); in the 1960 edition the word is corrupted by OCR. Perhaps শ্রীরাম পণ্ডিত (*śrīrāma paṇḍita*) should be read — Shrirama Pandita, Srivasa's brother. Reading unclear (OCR).
[^630]: Sri Chandrashekhara Acharya has already been named in 4053; so in all copies.
[^631]: মাধবেন্দ্রপুরীশ্বর (*mādhavendra-purīśvara*) — "the master Madhavendra Puri", as in 5.2025–2062; perhaps two persons are meant, Madhavendra Puri and Ishvara Puri.
[^632]: Vallabha (Anupama) is the younger brother of Sanatana and Rupa and the father of Jiva Gosvami.
[^633]: "Loses his composure": the 1912/13 edition (B) has আনালা অধৈর্য (*ānālā adhairya*), the 1960 edition সামনে অধ (*sāmane adha*); both are corrupted by OCR, understood as হইলা অধৈর্য (*hailā adhairya*, "became agitated"). Reading unclear (OCR).
